Normalization for Multimodal Type Theory
Daniel Gratzer

Abstract
. We consider the conversion problem for multimodal type theory ( MTT ) [ Gra+20 ] by characterizing the normal forms of the type theory and proving normalization ( Theorem 8.5 ). Normalization follows from a novel adaptation of Sterling’s Synthetic Tait Computability [ SH20 , Ste21 ] which generalizes the framework to accommodate a type theory with modalities and multiple modes. As a corollary of our main result, we reduce the conversion problem of MTT to the conversion problem of its mode theory ( Corollary 8.8 ) and show the injectivity of type constructors ( Corollary 8.9 ).



1.
Introduction

The last twenty years of development of type theory has seen many different extensions of type
theories to account for different modalities. Most of these type theories are
specialized—accounting for a specific collection of modalities—and intended for a particular
model. This specialization has allowed for concise and practicable syntax in some cases, but has
also created a tremendous amount of churn where even the smallest change to the modal situation
requires substantial work to produce a new type theory.

In response to this situation, several “frameworks” for modal type theory have been put
forward. A framework for modal type theory should enjoy the following:

(1)

sufficient generality to accommodate a large class of modalities,

(2)

practicable syntax in specific applications,

(3)

a wide variety of theorems which hold for instantiations.

There is a degree of tension between these three goals: a framework which is more general often
admits more cumbersome syntax, and wider classes of type theories typically enjoy fewer shared
properties. Recently,
[
Gra+20a
]
introduced
MTT
, a type theory parameterized by a
collection of modalities.
MTT
aims to support a highly usable syntax for any collection of
modalities which behave like (dependent) right adjoints
[
Bir+20
]
. This proves to be
a rich class of modalities, allowing encodings of type theories for guarded recursion, internal
parametricity, axiomatic cohesion, etc.

1.1.
MTT

We briefly recall some of details of
MTT
. For a full account, see the extended submission on

MTT

[
Gra+20
]
. As mentioned,
MTT
is parameterized by a collection of
modalities. Formally,
MTT
is defined over a
mode theory

[
LS16
]
: a strict
2-category whose objects represent modes, while 1-cells determine modalities and 2-cells natural
transformations between them.

Given a mode theory
\mathcal{M}
, each mode
m:\mathcal{M}
determines a mode in
MTT
. Each mode behaves
somewhat like its own type theory. There are separate judgments for each mode
\Gamma\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
,

\Gamma\vdash A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}
, and
\Gamma\vdash M:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
and types in mode
m
are closed under the standard connectives in
Martin-Löf type theory (dependent products, sums, booleans, an intensional identity type, and a
universe).

Different modes only interact through modalities. Given a morphism
\mu :\hbox to31.7pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.00117pt\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@stroke}}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
, there is an
induced modality
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
in
MTT
which sends types from mode
n
to mode
m
. This modality
behaves like a right adjoint
[
Bir+20
]
, whose left adjoint is given an action only
on contexts
-.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
.
1
1

1

Other presentations of
MTT
and Fitch-style modal type theories
write
-.\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{\mu}
for this action, but we find
-.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
more uniform and less cumbersome.

This operation is a proper functor between context categories, with an induced action on
substitutions
\gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. As the left adjoint,
-.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
sends context from mode
m
to
mode
n
. This left adjoint is used to give the formation and introduction rules for
MTT
by
transposition:

\frac{\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}}\frac{\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash M:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(M):\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

Two questions remain: how does one eliminate an element of
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and how can one use a
context of the form
\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. While related modal type theories solve both issues through
defining the elimination rule to be transposition in the opposite direction, this causes issues for
the substitution property. Instead,
MTT
employs an alternative approach. First, variables in the
context is annotated with a modality:

\frac{\Gamma\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}}{\Gamma.(\mu\mid A)\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

These modal annotations are used to determine when a variable can be accessed; a variable annotated
with
\mu
can be accessed precisely when it is behind
-.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
:

\frac{}{\Gamma.(\mu\mid A).\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\mathbf{v}_{0}:A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\uparrow}.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle{\uparrow}.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\uparrow}.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\uparrow}.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

Next, the elimination rule for
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is used to bridge the gap between a variable annotated
with
\nu\circ\mu
of type
A
and a variable annotated with
\nu
of type
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. There is
a canonical substitution
\Gamma.(\nu\circ\mu\mid A)
\Gamma.(\nu\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}})
, but a
priori no map in the reverse direction. While we do not provide an inverse outright, the elimination
rule ensures that these two contexts are isomorphic “from the perspective of a
type”. Semantically, one ensures that display maps are weakly right orthogonal to this map, but
more concretely we add the following elimination principle:

\frac{\nu :\hbox to27.77pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\quad\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}})\vdash B{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{m}}\quad\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash M_{0}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid M_{1}:B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle{{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\uparrow}}.{\mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{0})}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}_{\mu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\nu}(\,\underline{\phantom{-}}\,)\leftarrow M_{0}\ \mathsf{in}\ M_{1}:B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}.{M_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}

Finally, the interactions between modalities are codified by organizing the collection of functors

-.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
into a single 2-functor out of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{coop}}
. Concretely, this ensures that there
is a substitution
\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
for each
2-cell
\alpha :\hbox to27.88pt{\vbox to7.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-1.94443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.46991pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

2-functoriality is sufficient to ensure that the modal types organize into a weak 2-functor, with

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{id}_{m}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{id}_{m}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{id}_{m}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{id}_{m}\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

A
.

1.2.
Normalization for
MTT

Returning to the criterion for a framework for modal dependent type theories,

[
Gra+20
]
showed that
MTT
enjoys substantial metatheoretic properties. In
particular, every instantiation of
MTT
is sound and enjoys a canonicity result. Both of these
results are proven semantically, taking advantage of the fact that an instantiation of
MTT
comes
equipped with a well-behaved class of models in which syntax is initial. Notably, however, they
stopped short of showing that
MTT
enjoys a normalization result, and the lack of such a result
precludes a general approach to implementing instantiations of
MTT
.

Here, we rectify the situation and show that
MTT
admits a normalization result. As a corollary,
conversion in
MTT
is decidable when equality of modalities and 2-cells is decidable. This result
paves the way for an implementation of
MTT
which does not necessitate a full reimplementation for
each tweak of the modalities.

Normalization results for dependent type theories are typically involved affairs, requiring the
construction of a sophisticated PER model, families of logical relations, and other technical
constructions. For this proof, however, we adapt recent gluing arguments for normalization to give a
concise and detailed
proof
[
AHS95
,
Fio02
,
AK16
,
Coq19
,
Ste21
]
.

While the literature contains a large number of modal dependent type theories, very few are proven
to enjoy normalization. Of these, the most closely related is

MLTT

[
GSB19
]
. This is a type theory extending
MLTT
with an idempotent
comonad. However, compared to the normalization proof of
MLTT

given by

[
GSB19
]
, this result is significantly shorter and more conceptual.
2
2

2

As a
crude measurement, the proof of normalization for
MLTT

occupies the bulk of the
accompanying 90 page technical report
[
GSB19a
]
. The proof of
normalization for
MTT
, by contrast, takes approximately 25 pages to present with more detail.

Furthermore, the result for
MTT
applies to a wide variety of modal situations, including modal
type theory with an idempotent comonad.

1.3.
Proof outline

In broad strokes, the proof of normalization for
MTT
proceeds in several stages. First, we
introduce the technical device of
MTT
cosmoi in
Section

3
. An
MTT
cosmos is a more
flexible version of the models of
MTT
originally described. In particular, cosmoi are only
required to be locally Cartesian closed, so that there is no ‘fiberwise representability’
requirement, and morphisms between models are only required to preserve several structures up to
isomorphism. This definition of model is more closely related to the presentations of type theories
as
representable map categories

[
Uem19
]
or
locally Cartesian closed
categories

[
GS20
]
. This flexibility is essential for the application of
synthetic Tait computability and allows us to avoid tedious calculations.

Remark 1
.

We emphasize, however, this change of categories is a technical detail of the proof: the
normalization function is defined on the stricter syntax constituting an initial object of the CwF
models as originally defined rather than the initial object of the category of cosmoi. While it is
more work to obtain a normalization result of this form, it ensures that one does not need to
accept the initial
MTT
cosmos as syntax; such acceptance would require a leap of faith, because
there is no adequacy result for
MTT
cosmoi corresponding those proven by
[
Uem19
]

and
[
GS20
]
. Such an adequacy result is difficult to formulate for
MTT

cosmoi because of the lack of established modal logical frameworks.

In
Section

4
we introduce the necessary mathematics to generalize synthetic Tait
computability (STC) from working internally to a single gluing category to applying to a collection
of gluing categories interconnected by functors and natural transformations. In particular, we show
that one may work internally to this network of categories in
MTT
, using
MTT
modalities to
pass between gluing categories. We also show that the fibered modalities available in each gluing
category commute with modalities already present in
MTT
, a crucial result for constructing the
normalization model.

With this framework in place,
Section

5
and
Section

6
define the basic objects of
the normalization model: renamings, normal and neutral forms, etc. In particular,

Section

6
defines the gluing categories used for normalization, along with the functors
between them. This portion of the proof proceeds with minimal alternations from a standard proof of
normalization for
MLTT
. The only changes are to account for multiple modalities, and this change
is mostly one of book-keeping. One novelty of
Section

5
is the usage of a non-free
presentation of renamings. By allowing renamings to enjoy non-trivial equations, we vastly simplify
the development. As a consequence of this decision, we only must show that the action of a renaming
on a normal form respects these equations — a straightforward calculation.

The actual construction of the normalization model, and the heart of the proof, takes place in

Section

7
. In particular, we define an
MTT
cosmos of glued categories
together with the reify and reflect maps needed to define the normalization function. Those familiar
with proofs of normalization by gluing, and in particular with those given by STC will find much of
the construction familiar. The central challenge is the construction of modal types in the
normalization model (
Lemma

7.6
), and in particular the construction of the
modal elimination law. Other connectives which do not interact with modalities (booleans, universes,
dependent sums, etc.) can be constructed in this multimodal setting with no alteration.

Finally, the normalization function is extracted from this model in
Section

8
. Taking
advantage of the fact that this proof is constructive, we also observe that the normalization
function is effective. This uses a novel approach to derive a normalization result for strict
syntax, rather than the initial
MTT
cosmos. Using these results, we establish several other
corollaries such as the decidability of equality and the injectivity of type-constructors.

2.
Normalization via Synthetic Tait Computability

The proof of normalization for
MTT
follows from a systematic generalization of Synthetic Tait
Computability (STC) to multimodal type theories. While a full introduction of the theory of STC is
beyond the scope of this work, given that a comprehensive account has not yet emerged
3
3

3

We
hope that the forthcoming work of
[
Ste21
]
will serve this role
we give a short
summary of the key ideas here. We focus in particular on proving normalization via STC. Other
descriptions of STC are given by
[
SH20
]
,
[
SG20
]
, and

[
SA21
]
.

Prior to discussing STC, we remark that in the process of embracing STC we are naturally led to a
number of ideas which are not intrinsically categorical but still crucial to the concision of the
proof.

•

Our normalization proof is
reduction-free
, and

•

the algorithm works only over equivalences classes of well-typed terms.

More explicitly, our proof is reduction-free in that it does not proceed by fixing a rewriting
system presenting the equational theory which we then prove to be confluent and strongly
normalizing. This avoids the thorny issue of finding an rewriting system which faithfully presents
the equational theory of
MTT
—the inclusion of
\eta
-laws for dependent sums greatly
complicates such a task.

To the second point, working with terms only up to definitional equality obviates the need for a
separate proof of
completeness
of the normalization algorithm. More than this, it is also a
necessary step to treating
MTT
categorically; without this quotienting, the category of contexts
is not even a category. Similarly, the universal properties of various connectives of
MTT
are
crucially leveraged to simplify aspects of the normalization proof, but these universal properties
only come into being after taking terms up to definitional equality.

2.1.
Normalization by gluing

Proving normalization via STC involves working internally to a particular glued category. This idea
branches off the observation that that logical relations arguments could be systematically recast as
the construction of a model of type theory in a
Freyd cover
or
Sierpinski cone
(scone)

[
MS93
]
. This is a special case of the more general gluing

.

}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 10.18 3.46 L 24.73 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{7.35408pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0.5 0 C 1.6 0 2.49 -1.11 2.49 -1.99 C 2.49 -3.1 1.6 -3.98 0.5 -3.98}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.07079pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
, but this is both less categorically natural
and, as we shall see in later, counterproductive.

What makes this shift in perspective so useful is the remarkable ability of
\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{C})
to
seemingly inherit all the structure of
\mathcal{C}
in such a way that the projection

\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{C})
\mathcal{C}
preserves this structure. For instance, if
\mathcal{C}
is Cartesian closed, then
so too is
\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{C})
and the projection preserves all this structure. In fact, an explicit
construction of e.g., the exponential in
\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{C})
yields almost precisely the standard
construction of the logical relation at function type. The benefit of relying on such technology,
however, is that one can typically avoid these explicit calculations; once it is known that

\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{C})
is Cartesian closed, canonicity at ground type can be proven without ever needing to
explicitly calculate the construction of exponentials.

The correspondence between logical relations and gluing was pushed further in multiple
directions
[
AHS95
,
Str98
,
Alt+01
,
Fio02
]
. In particular, the
insistence of logical relations of
closed terms
of a type can be relaxed by switching to a
different comma category. We are interested in proving normalization, emphatically not a result
solely concerned with closed terms.

Accordingly, rather than considering a model in
\mathbf{S}(\mathcal{C})
, we will consider a glued category
valued in presheaves over renamings. It is perhaps more natural to expect one to examine presheaves
over contexts: after all, terms organize into a presheaf over arbitrary contexts and substitutions,
with substitution giving rise to the reindexing action of the presheaf. One would then consider a
model in
\mathbf{Gl}(\mathbf{y})
; the category given by gluing along the Yoneda embedding.

The first attempts at categorical proofs of normalization constructed models in
\mathbf{Gl}(\mathbf{y})
, so that
one considered predicates not on the closed terms of type
A
, but over the family

(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,A$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\Gamma,A$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Gamma,A$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma,A$}\mathclose{}{]}})_{\Gamma}
. The presheaf condition corresponds to the monotonicity conditions
familiar to all Kripke logical relations, but in particular those for normalization. Unfortunately,
these presheaf conditions are too onerous to view normal forms as a presheaf on contexts — normal
forms are never stable under all substitutions — so that the resultant normalization function is
given as a map from terms to terms and indistinguishable from the identity function on terms.

This can be rectified taking presheaves over the wide subcategory of renamings
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
. By cutting down the substitutions allowed between contexts, normal forms may be organized into
an object of
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{R})

[
AHS95
,
Fio02
]
. Accordingly, normalization proceeds
not by gluing along the Yoneda lemma as still repeated in literature, but by gluing along a
restriction of the Yoneda lemma, the nerve
\mathbf{n}:\hbox to59.92pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.61111pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.61111pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.52774pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.76 3.46 L 25.08 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.32774pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
. Unlike canonicity, it is
insufficient to simply construct a model of
\mathbb{T}
in
\mathbf{Gl}(\mathbf{n})
, extra structure is required which
‘sandwiches’ the predicate for a type
A
between the neutral and normal forms of
A
, so that the
following commutative diagram exists in
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{R})
:

(1)

\mathsf{Ne}_{A}
P_{A}
\mathsf{Nf}_{A}
\mathbf{n}(A)
{\uparrow}_{A}
{\downarrow}_{A}

The top two maps of this diagram are categorical realizations of the
reflect
and
reify

maps found in normalization-by-evaluation
[
Abe13
]
and are used to extract the normalization
function. In the simply-typed case, these maps can be constructed by induction after the fact, but
in the dependently-typed setting this approach does not scale and the two must be constructed
simultaneously. Accordingly, we require a type in our glued model to be not just a proof-relevant
predicate
P_{A}
over
\mathbf{n}(A)
, but a triple of

(P_{A},{\uparrow}_{A},{\downarrow}_{A})

[
AHS95
,
Fio02
]
.

One final remark is needed prior to the introduction of STC. Thus far we have limited consideration
to simply-typed languages. The full utility of proof-relevance only becomes apparent when
considering dependent type theory with universes. In these cases, it becomes necessary to associate
with an element of a universe not merely a proposition (computable or not computable) but to instead
pair an element with the
computability data of a type
again. This is beautifully handled by
the proof-relevance of predicates: the predicate on universes at
A:\mathsf{U}
contains choices of
computability data over
\mathsf{El}(A)
as witnesses
[
Shu15
,
Coq19
]
.

2.2.
Synthetic Tait Computability

Finally, we now turn to Synthetic Tait Computability. STC builds on the idea of working with glued
categories, but instead of gluing together locally Cartesian closed categories presenting dependent
type theories
[
GS20
]
, STC is concerned with topoi. Suppose
\mathbb{T}
presents a
type theory, and
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
is the subcategory of renamings. Rather than gluing together
the locally Cartesian closed categories
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{R})
along
\mathbf{n}
, we instead glue together

\mathbf{PSh}(\mathbb{T})
and
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{R})
along the inverse image of the essentially geometric morphism induced by
the inclusion
i:\hbox to31.5pt{\vbox to6.93pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.68056pt\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.59718pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.86 3.46 L 25.18 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.39719pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
. As topoi are closed under gluing, the resultant glued category

\mathbf{Gl}(i)
is a topos. In fact, by a standard result
[
AGV72
,
CJ95
]
it is a presheaf
topos.

As a result,
\mathbf{Gl}(i)
supports a model of extensional Martin-Löf type theory with a hierarchy of
cumulative universes. Inside this type theory, two fibered modalities,
\fullmoon
and
\newmoon
, allow
us to recover
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathbb{T})
and
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{R})
inside of
\mathbf{Gl}(i)
. By relaxing the definition of a model
of Martin-Löf type theory to non-representability universes moreover, we may specify and
construct the glued model purely internally to
\mathbf{Gl}(i)
.

This internal approach offers a significant improvement over previous proofs of gluing for dependent
type theory
[
Coq19
,
KHS19
]
which work externally and incur what

[
Ste21
]
has referred to as an ‘avalanche’ of naturality obligations. As we shall see
in
Section

7
, these tedious calculations are replaced with programming
exercises in extensional type theory (or
MTT
in our case). In particular, a model lying over the
model in
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathbb{T})
corresponds to a series of constants subject to the requirement that they are
sent by
\fullmoon
to the constants defining the model in
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathbb{T})
.

We refer to the internal type theory of
\mathbf{Gl}(i)
—extensional type theory with cumulative
universes, two fibered modalities, and a few axioms governing their behavior—as the language of
Synthetic Tait Computability.

3.
MTT
Cosmoi

While the basic theory of models of
MTT
is detailed by
[
Gra+20
]
, we
generalize to locally Cartesian closed categories. This change, inspired by

[
GS20
]
, is crucial to actually carrying out the normalization theorem as it
removes
representability requirements
from the universes of types and terms. These prove
superfluous for the theorem, and forcing the glued model to satisfy them is both unnatural and
surprisingly challenging.

Remark 2
.

Henceforth, we shall consider
MTT
over a fixed mode theory
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 3.2
.

The 2-category
\mathbf{Cat}_{g}
consists of small categories, functors between them, and invertible
natural transformations.

Definition 3.3
.

The locally full 2-subcategory
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is spanned by pseudofunctors
F

enjoying the following two properties:

•

For each
m:\mathcal{M}
,
F(m)
is a locally Cartesian closed category.

•

For each
\mu :\hbox to31.7pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.00117pt\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

(4)

\tau_{\mu}
contains a subuniverse also closed under all these connectives.

To a first approximation, an
MTT
cosmos is a standard model of
MTT
formulated in the language
in natural models without the requirements that the universes be legitimate representable natural
transformations. By expanding the class of models in this way, we make it far easier to construct
the glued model in
Section

7
.

Remark 3
.

In order to simplify matters, we assume
MTT
has only a weak universe à la Tarski. Concretely,
there exists a universe
\mathsf{U}
and a decoding operation
\mathsf{El}(-)
just as in

[
Gra+20
]
. Unlike the original presentation of
MTT
however, we only
obtain a chosen isomorphism between each connective and its decoded code e.g.

\mathsf{dec}^{\!\rhd}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathsf{El}(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathrel{\widehat{\to}}B)\cong\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid\mathsf{El}(A)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{El}(A)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{El}(A)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{El}(A)$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{El}(B)
.

Recent unpublished work by Gratzer and Sterling has shown that Synthetic Tait
Computability
[
Ste21
]
can be strengthened to accommodate stricter universes,
and can be applied to the following without issue. We remark, however, that there is experimental
evidence that elaboration can be used to alleviate the tedium of these weaker universes in
practice
[
Red20
]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Any strict model of
MTT
induces a cosmos. In particular, the initial model of
MTT

\mathcal{S}\llbracket-\rrbracket
induces a cosmos.

Remark 4
.

We have used ‘syntactic’ notation (
\Gamma\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
,
\Gamma\vdash M:A{}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, etc.) to denote the
components of the initial model
\mathcal{S}\llbracket-\rrbracket
. This is a mere notational convenience: we do not
rely on the fact that
\mathcal{S}\llbracket-\rrbracket
may be constructed out of ‘traditional’ syntax.

Proof.

For clarity, we write
\mathbf{El}_{m}
,
\mathbf{Ty}_{m}
and
\mathbf{Tm}_{m}
instead of
\tau_{m}
,
\mathcal{T}_{m}
, and
\tau_{m}
in the
syntactic model, reserving the latter exclusively for
G
. We write
\llbracket\mu\rrbracket
for the functor
sending
\Gamma
to
\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. We may replace
G
by an equivalent strict 2-functor such
that
\pi
becomes strictly 2-natural, so we work under this assumption.

We construct a displayed model of
MTT

[
KKA19
]
which lies over the
syntactic model.

m
are interpreted by triples

(X:G(m),\Gamma,\alpha:\pi(X)\cong\mathbf{y}(\Gamma))
.

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
and
\Gamma\vdash\delta:\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
such that

\beta\circ\pi(f)=\mathbf{y}(\delta)\circ\alpha

Once this model is constructed, the result is a direct consequence of the initiality of (strict)
syntax. In fact, the construction of contexts, substitutions, terms, and types is relatively
routine owing to the fact that
\pi
is a 2-natural transformation, preserves finite limits,
and strictly commutes with all connectives. We show a few cases to give a flavor for the
procedure.

(X,\Gamma,\alpha)

, we define the

while

.

, we form the context

(X\times_{G(\mu)(\mathcal{T}_{m})}G(\dot{\mathcal{T}}_{m}),\Gamma.(\mu\mid A),\beta)
, where

\beta
is the composite:

\pi(X\times_{G(\mu)(\mathcal{T}_{m})}G(\mu)(\dot{\mathcal{T}}_{m}))\cong\pi(X)\times_{\llbracket\mu\rrbracket^{*}(\mathbf{Ty}_{m})}\llbracket\mu\rrbracket^{*}(\mathbf{Tm}_{m})\cong\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\mid A))

The first isomorphism follows from the fact that
\pi
preserves finite limits, is strictly
2-natural, and strictly preserves
\tau_{m}
while the second isomorphism is the universal property
of
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.(\mu\mid A))
.

Modal types

Suppose we are given a context
(X,\Gamma,\alpha)
and a type
(A^{*},A)
in the context

(G(\mu)_{{!}}(\mu)(X),\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},\gamma\circ\llbracket\mu\rrbracket^{*}(\alpha)\circ\beta)
. We
form the modal type as

(\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(\widehat{A^{*}}),\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}})

It remains to check that these types are coherent. That is, that

\pi(\mathbf{Mod}_{\mu}(\widehat{A^{*}}))=\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\rfloor}{\lfloor\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\rfloor}\circ\alpha

By assumption,
\pi(A^{*})=\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}\circ\gamma\circ\llbracket\mu\rrbracket^{*}(\alpha)\circ\beta
. By our
assumption that
\pi
commutes with transposition,

\pi(\widehat{A^{*}})=\widehat{\mathchoice{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\left\lfloor\mkern-2.0mu{A}\mkern-2.0mu\right\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}{\lfloor A\rfloor}\circ\gamma}\circ\alpha
. The result follows from
the fact that
\pi
preserves
\mathbf{Mod}
.
∎

3.1.
An initial cosmos

While the following material is not strictly necessary for our proof of normalization, we record it
for general interest.

Theorem 3.8
.

The category of MTT cosmoi can be sketched over
\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

First we show that
\mathcal{V}
is 2-monadic over
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
. This follows directly
from the fact that locally Cartesian closure can be realized as a finitary 2-monad on

\mathbf{Cat}_{g}

[
Lac09
, Section 5.9]
and pseudofunctors whose 1-cells are right adjoints
are finitarily 2-monadic over
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}

[
Lac09
, Section
5.9]
. Combining the operations and equations shows
\mathcal{V}
is finitarily 2-monadic over

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}}{\mathopen{}{|}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{|}},\mathbf{Cat}_{g}$}\mathclose{}{]}}
.

The constants and equations of
MTT
-cosmoi are sketchable over this 2-monad following the work
of
[
KPT99
]
.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

The category of
MTT
-cosmoi has a bi-initial object
\mathcal{I}\llbracket-\rrbracket
.

4.
Foundations of multimodal Synthetic Tait Computability

Eventually, we will wish to work with a collection of models of Synthetic Tait Computability (STC),
connected by a variety of adjunctions. In order for this to work, however, we need to show that
adjunctions between various categories lift to adjunctions between glued categories. The main
result of this section is
Theorem

4.13
, which states that a collection of glued
categories interconnected by right adjoints supports a model of “multimodal” STC.

.

\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{1})

\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{0})
\mathcal{X}_{0}
\mathcal{Y}_{0}

Moreover, if both fringe functors
\rho_{i}
are continuous and
f
and

are essential, then

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is also essential.

Colimits and finite limits are determined pointwise in
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{i}))

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}^{*}
preserves them because
f^{*}
and
g^{*}
both do. If both
\rho_{i}

are continuous, then all limits are determined pointwise, so
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is essential if both

f
and
g
are.
∎

.

. Given that

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{!}
and

are defined pointwise, it suffices to show that
the transpose operators for

and
g_{!}\dashv g^{*}
lift.

such that

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}(\widehat{b})\circ\epsilon\circ g_{!}(\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(\eta))\circ g_{!}(x)

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}(\widehat{b})\circ\epsilon\circ g_{!}(\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(\eta)\circ x)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\circ g_{!}(g^{*}(\rho_{0}(\widehat{b}))\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(\eta)\circ x)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\circ g_{!}(\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(f^{*}(\widehat{b}))\circ\rho_{1}(\eta)\circ x)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\circ g_{!}(\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(b)\circ x)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\circ g_{!}(g^{*}(x^{\prime})\circ a)

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}\circ\widehat{a}

\alpha\circ g^{*}(x^{\prime})\circ\widehat{a}=\rho_{1}(\widehat{b})\circ x

\displaystyle\alpha\circ g^{*}(x^{\prime})\circ\widehat{a}

\displaystyle=\alpha\circ g^{*}(x^{\prime})\circ g^{*}(a)\circ\eta

\displaystyle=\alpha\circ g^{*}(x^{\prime}\circ a)\circ\eta

\displaystyle=\alpha\circ g^{*}(\rho_{0}(b)\circ\tilde{x})\circ\eta

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}(f^{*}(b))\circ\alpha\circ g^{*}(\epsilon\circ g_{!}(\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(\eta)\circ x))\circ\eta

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}(f^{*}(b))\circ\alpha\circ g^{*}(\epsilon)\circ\eta\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\rho_{1}(\eta)\circ x

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}(f^{*}(b))\circ\rho_{1}(\eta)\circ x

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}(\widehat{b})\circ x\qed

It is natural to view
\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{i})
as a topos with a subterminal object
\mathbf{syn}_{i}
such that the
open subtopos corresponding to
\mathbf{syn}_{i}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{X}_{i}
, while the closed subtopos is
equivalent to
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
. For the sake of easy of readability, we will identify these subtopoi with

\mathcal{X}_{i}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
in what follows. Recall that the inverse image of the inclusion

\mathcal{X}_{i}
\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{i})
is given by
-^{\mathbf{syn}_{i}}
. The inverse image of

\mathcal{Y}_{i}
\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{i})
is given by
-\mathbin{\star}\mathbf{syn}_{i}
, where
A\mathbin{\star}\mathbf{syn}_{i}
is the
pushout
A\mathbin{\textstyle\coprod_{A\times\mathbf{syn}_{i}}}\mathbf{syn}_{i}
.

4.1.
Open and closed subtopoi

We now devote substantial effort to showing that the decomposition of
\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{i})
is preserved by

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
A:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{0}))
is contained in
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{Y}_{0})
, then
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}(A):\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})
.

Proof.

In fact, a more general theorem is true. Reflecting an object into either subtopos and applying

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is (naturally) isomorphic to applying
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
and then reflecting.

Corollary 4.6
.

(1)

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}(\mathbf{syn}_{0})\cong\mathbf{syn}_{1}

(2)

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-^{\mathbf{syn}_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-^{\mathbf{syn}_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-^{\mathbf{syn}_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-^{\mathbf{syn}_{0}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\cong\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mathbf{syn}_{1}}

(3)

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}(\mathbf{syn}_{0}\mathbin{\star}{}-)\cong\mathbf{syn}_{1}\mathbin{\star}{}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Remark 5
.

The last fact of
Corollary

4.6
is a consequence of the first;

\mathbin{\star}
is preserved by
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
because as this functor is lex and cocontinuous and

\mathbin{\star}
is defined by finite products and pushouts. Exponentiation, however, is not generally
preserved by
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
. It is a special fact of
\mathbf{syn}_{0}
that exponentiation by
\mathbf{syn}_{0}

is preserved.

As idempotent lex modalities, both of these actions internalize into the internal type theory of the
topoi. In particular, they have actions on a family given by applying the operation to the entire
family, then pulling back along the unit. This is automatically preserved by
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}^{*}
,
as
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}^{*}
preserves each step in this construction. We write
\fullmoon
for the action
of
-^{\mathbf{syn}_{0}}
on families, and
\newmoon
for
-\mathbin{\star}\mathbf{syn}_{0}
. We will abusively use the same
notation for
-^{\mathbf{syn}_{0}}
and
-^{\mathbf{syn}_{1}}
as well as
-\mathbin{\star}\mathbf{syn}_{0}
and
-\mathbin{\star}\mathbf{syn}_{1}
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Both
\fullmoon
and
\newmoon
are preserved by
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}^{*}

We would like to show a slightly stronger result, namely that the dependent versions of these
modalities are preserved
[
RSS20
]
. Prior to this, we must address the
question of universes and models of type theory in this situation.

As a morphism of logoi, we know that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is a cocontinuous left exact functor. We would
like to show that it induces a dependent right adjoint
[
Bir+20
]
, but this is
complicated by the murky definition of a model of dependent type theory with universes in an
arbitrary logos. Accordingly, prove a theorem which is sufficient for the case we have in mind.

Definition 4.8
.

A functor
F:\hbox to88.61pt{\vbox to10pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.8194pt\lower-2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.8194pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

Remark 6
.

To be precise, we must specify the particular models of dependent type theory we consider in

\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D})
. We take the model of type theory which a type to be an element of the
universe of small presheaves
[
HS97
]
.

Proof.

Fix a Grothendieck universe
\mathcal{V}
which is large enough to contain both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
. We wish
to show that
F
induces a weak CwF morphism. A type in
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C})
is given by a generalized
element of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the Hofmann-Streicher universe of
\mathcal{V}
-small presheaves, so it suffices to
show that there is a pullback square of the following shape:

F(\dot{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{C}})
F(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}})
F(\tau_{\mathcal{C}})
\dot{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{D}}
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{D}}
\tau_{\mathcal{D}}

Proving this is equivalent to showing that
F(\tau_{\mathcal{C}})
is
\mathcal{U}
-small because
\tau_{\mathcal{D}}
is
generic for such maps. This immediately defines a weak CwF morphism using the observation that
context extension in these models is defined by pullback.
∎

While these results are limited to presheaf topoi, they are sufficient for our purposes; we are
interested in gluing together presheaf topoi along a continuous functor. The following result shows
that the resultant topos is of presheaf type:

Theorem 4.11

(
[
AGV72
,
CJ95
]
)
.

Gluing together presheaf topoi along a continuous functor results in a presheaf topos.

Motivated by this, we now assume that
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{F}_{i}
are all presheaf logoi, and all morphisms
involved preserve all Grothendieck universes larger than
\mathcal{U}
. In this case, we may then pick a
Grothendieck universe
\mathcal{U}
large enough to be preserved by both
f
and
g
and so the bases of

\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{1})
and
\mathbf{Gl}(\rho_{1})
are small for this universe. In this case,
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
is a DRA
which preserves all universes larger than
\mathcal{U}
.

We now show that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
preserves the dependent version of
\fullmoon
and

\newmoon
.

Theorem 4.12
.

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}^{*}
preserves
\hat{\textnormal{$\fullmoon$}}
and
\hat{\textnormal{$\newmoon$}}
on any universe preserved by

We will show this for

and indeed
for any lex modality preserved by

4.2.
Interpreting
MTT
in glued topoi

The results of the previous subsection show that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
preserves the open and closed
modalities of a glued topos. As a dependent right adjoint, moreover,
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{]}}
can be
internalized into a model of
MTT
. It is this model of
MTT
that we use to substantiate the
language of ‘multimodal’ synthetic Tait computability.

m
is equivalent to
\llbracket m\times 0\rrbracket
,
(resp.
\llbracket m\times 1\rrbracket
).

In this model, each mode supports a model of synthetic Tait computability and the extra structure of
this language (the open and closed modalities) is preserved by the modalities between modes.

5.
The category of renamings for
MTT

We now isolate a class of renamings:
\mathsf{Ren}_{m}
. We also define the neutral and normal forms of terms
in
MTT
. Unlike the syntax of
MTT
, neutral and normal forms are not taken up to a complex
equivalence relation, and so their conversion problem is immediately reducible to conversion in the
mode theory.

The judgments for these are slightly atypical, in that the typing judgments for both neutral and
normal forms are defined (1) with formal telescopes as contexts and (2) inductive-recursively with
the inclusion of neutral and normals into terms, and renamings into contexts and substitutions.
This dependence of the unquotiented raw syntax on actual terms is necessary in order to ensure that
the typing relation can be sensibly defined.

The full collection of neutral and normal forms is given in

Appendix

A
. We present a few representative cases below.

\framebox{$\displaystyle\Theta\ \mathsf{tele}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\rightsquigarrow\Gamma\qquad\Theta\vdash\psi:\Psi{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\rightsquigarrow\gamma$}\\
\frac{\Theta\ \mathsf{tele}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}\rightsquigarrow\Gamma}{\Theta.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\ There are no equations imposed on any of these generators, except for renamings which have the
necessary equations to organize them into a 2-functor into
\mathbf{Cat}
. We further define a substitution
action applying a renaming to a normal or neutral form, which must show respects these
equations. This operation and its pertinent proofs are largely standard, with the exception being
the case for variables. We reproduce the definition of the substitution action on variables here:

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\alpha\circ\beta}

Remark 7
.

Note that these normal forms do not necessarily enjoy decidable equality. Rather, the problem of
deciding when two normal forms are convertible is precisely the problem of deciding whether
certain 1- and 2-cells of the mode theory
\mathcal{M}
are equal. Accordingly, it is possible that

MTT
may enjoy normalization, but not decidable type-checking. The mode theories used in
instantiations of
MTT
thus far are evidently decidable, so we do not expect this to be an issue
for practical usage.

6.
Prerequisites for the normalization model

Key syntactic categories

Section

\mathsf{Ren}_{m}
